 1

 Executive summary

In this DOE-funded project DE-SC0010081-020 Old Dominion University (ODU) in collaboration with
University of Wisconsin (UW) and Jefferson Laboratory have investigated both experimentally and
theoretically electromagnetic response and losses in multilayered superconducting structures made of new
SRF materials which can push the field and Q performance limits of accelerating cavities.

The project was comprised of the following main thrusts:

 1. Growth and optimization of S-I-S multilayers, particularly Nb3Sn/Al2O3/Nb3Sn multilayers (UW
 group of Chang-Beom Eom (co-PI), graduate student Chris Sundahl and postdocs).
 2. Electromagnetic characterization of Nb3Sn films and multilayers (Alex Gurevich (Pi), Jean
 Delayen (co-PI), Paul Welander (SLAC), Gianluigi Ciovati (JLab), Pashupati Dhakal (JLab),
 graduate students Junki Makita and Harshani Senevirathne.
 3. Theory development (Alex Gurevich (PI), Takauki Kubo (visiting scholar), postdoc Ahmad
 Sheikhzada and graduate students Ahmad Sheikhzada and Manula Pathirana.

In the course of this project five graduate students have received their PhD degrees:

1. Ahmad Sheikhzada. Nonlinear dynamics of vortices in different types of grain boundaries, May 2017,
 ODU (adv. A. Gurevich).
2. Christopher Sundahl. Synthesis of superconducting Nb3Sn thin film heterostructures for the study of
 high-energy RF physics, November 2019, UW (adv. C-B Eom).
3. W. P. Manula Randhika Pathirana. Numerical calculation of losses of trapped vortices under strong
 RF Meissner current and DC superheating field in type-II superconductors, April 2021, ODU (adv. A.
 Gurevich).
4. Junki Makita. Multi-technique characterization of superconducting materials for particle accelerator
 applications. April 2022, ODU (adv. A. Gurevich).
5. Iresha Harshani Senevirathne. Measurements of magnetic field penetration of materials for radio
 frequency superconducting cavities. May 2023, ODU (adv. J. Delayen).

The project has resulted in 27 journal publications and 23 contributions in conference proceedings.
Different parts of this work have been presented by PIs, postdocs and graduate students at International
Conference on RF Superconductivity, International Workshop on Thin Films and New Ideas for Pushing
the Limits of RF Superconductivity, Applied Superconductivity Conferences, European Conference on
Applied Superconductivity, TESLA Collaborative meetings, International Workshops on Vortex Matter in
Superconductors, March and April Meetings of the American Physical Society, International Particle
Accelerator Conferences and others (see Appendix 1 for a full list). Recent reprints of journal publications
are appended.

Detail reports on each of these main thrusts are given below.

 2

 THRUST 1. Growth and optimization of S-I-S multilayers

 Growth of Nb3Sn films and multilayers and their
 materials characterization

 The UW team has advanced a technology of growing
 Nb3Sn films and Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers onto 2” Al2O3
 wafers and Nb substrates suitable for the RF tests and
 patterning into stripline resonators. A representative
 geometry of multilayer samples deposited on a 200 nm
 thick Nb substrate is shown in Fig. 1.

 Shown in Fig. 2 is a schematic of Nb3Sn thin film
 deposition setup used by the UW group to produce
 Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers grown on 2” Al2O3 wafers.
 These multilayers typically had the transition temperature
 Tc » 17-17.6 K close to the maximum Tc = 18.3 K of
 stoichiometric Nb3Sn. Both single films and S-I-S
 Fig. 1. Schematic of Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayer
 heterostructures on Al2O3 wafer. Back side of
 multilayers with thickness from 50 to 500 nm have been
 Al2O3wafer is coated with a thick Nb film grown. Details are given in Refs. [1,2].

 Fig. 2. Schematic of thin films deposition setup and sequential processing steps for Nb3Sn/ Al2O3 multilayer heterostructures.
 (a) Nb and Sn are sputtered onto Al2O3 substrate while heater element is powered on. (b) After allowing film to cool, Al2O3 is
 sputtered from a single stoichiometric Al2O3 target. Sample is heated again to anneal Al2O3. These two steps are repeated to
 produce multilayer samples.

A series of films was grown with fixed Nb flux (0.7 Å/s) and varying Sn flux (0.4–2.5 Å/s), and low-
temperature resistance measurements were carried out to find the window for this self-regulating
adsorption-controlled process. Given the dependence of Tc on Sn content in Nb3Sn, this growth rate roughly
corresponds to the boundary between two processing regimes. At lower flux, Sn evaporates from the film
faster than it can be incorporated, resulting in a Sn-deficient film. At higher flux, sufficient Sn is provided
to react with all available Nb, and only excess Sn re-evaporates. The dielectric Al2O3 interlayers were grown
after allowing Nb3Sn to cool down to < 400 C, using a single stoichiometric target with RF power at a rate

 3

 Fig. 3. Cross-sectional transmission electron microscopy images of Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayer heterostructures. (a) Low-
 magnification image of trilayer morphology. (b) EDS compositional mapping of Al and O showing no interdiffusion between
 Al2O3 and Nb3Sn. (c) High-magnification image of the interfaces between Al2O3 and Nb3Sn

of 1.8 nm/min without any further heating applied to the substrate. Depositing under these conditions
protects the SiC heater element from oxygen evolved during the sputtering process and prevents undesired
reactions with the Nb3Sn surface. This Nb3Sn/Al2O3 stack was then heated again to above 900 C, which
allows the Al2O3 to crystallize, and the process was repeated to grow heterostructures of up to three Nb3Sn
layers as shown in Fig. 2.

Shown in Fig. 3 is SIS sample with three Nb3Sn layers prepared for analysis by cross-sectional scanning
transmission electron microscopy (STEM). A low-magnification image (Fig. 3a) represents the morphology
and nanostructure of the stack. Each Nb3Sn layer is polycrystalline with irregular interfaces and grain size
is 20–100 nm along the film surface direction. The Al2O3 layers conform closely to the layer below but are
discontinuous along the Nb3Sn/Al2O3 interface. Despite the repeated thermal cycling during stacking, it
appears that the lower layers have not degraded in comparison to the top layer. The chemical stability of
these films is further confirmed by compositional mapping with energy dispersive spectroscopy (EDS) (Fig.
3b). Intensity of the Al Kα and O Kα peaks are mapped from the region shown on the left. Although the
Al2O3 layers are not continuous, Al and O are confined to the Al2O3 layers, and do not mix with the Nb3Sn
layers. The exception to this is the presence of O at the interface of the topmost Nb3Sn film with the
atmosphere, where ambient conditions are sufficient to cause a reaction. A capping Al2O3 layer could be
deposited to prevent this oxidation, but we did not use it for the multilayer samples described in this work.
The slight O signal in the Nb3Sn layers in Fig. 3b is due to the slight oxidation of the TEM specimen surface.
As our RF cavity measurements show, these Al2O3 layers do not contribute significantly to surface
resistance at low fields. A higher-magnification image of the S–I interface is shown in Fig. 3c. The atomic
structure of Nb3Sn is well preserved at the interface, suggesting that there is almost no diffusion or
intermixing from the Al2O3. The lower Nb3Sn grain orients the [023] direction normal to the film surface,
and this direction is also preserved in the upper Nb3Sn grain. This can occur when the upper Nb3Sn layer
deposits with the same epitaxial relationship to the underlying Al2O3 as the lower layer has with the Al2O3
substrate. This structure can also form when a Nb3Sn grain nucleates on top of a Nb3Sn surface exposed by
breaks in the discontinuous Al2O3 layer. X-ray diffractometry indicates that Nb3Sn grains in the second
layer have more random crystallographic orientation compared to the first layer.

 4

 Our dc transport measurements have shown that
 the Nb3Sn films capped with Al2O3 and annealed
 with no further deposition exhibit good
 superconducting properties. For instance, the
 superconducting resistive transitions of a bare
 Nb3Sn film and a Nb3Sn/Al2O3 structure
 annealed at 900 C for 10 min are shown in Fig.
 4a. Here the critical temperature of the annealed
 sample is about 0.25 K higher than Tc of the
 unannealed sample, and residual resistivity ratio
 (RRR), an indicator of crystalline and metallic
 quality, is improved from 3.5 to 4.26. On the
 other hand, Nb3Sn films annealed without the
 Al2O3 cap, even under high Sn flux to prevent
 evaporative loss, have degraded Tc as compared
 to an un-annealed film. The superconducting
 properties essential for the RF performance were
 characterized by scanning tunneling
 spectroscopy (STS) which measures the
 differential tunneling conductance dI/dV
 proportional to the quasiparticle density of states
 (DOS), N(E). Shown in Fig. 4b is a
 representative tunneling spectrum measured in
 the center of a Nb3Sn grain at 4 K. The DOS
 curves, which clearly show the superconducting
 gap Δ at the Fermi surface, were fit using the
 conventional Dynes model [3,4]:
 Fig. 4: Properties of single-layer Nb3Sn films. (a) Resistive
 superconducting transition for two 60 nm thick films, one of 𝑁! (𝐸 − 𝑖Γ)
 which is capped with 6 nm Al2O3 overlayer and annealed at 900 𝑁(𝐸) = 𝑅𝑒
 °C for 10 minutes. (b) Density of states measured by scanning ,(𝐸 − 𝑖Γ)" − Δ"
 tunneling microscopy and Dynes fit for a 60 nm thick Nb3Sn film.
 where the Dynes parameter Γ accounts for the
broadening of the DOS peaks due to a finite lifetime of quasiparticles, and N0 is the DOS in the normal
state. The fit yields Γ = 0.36 meV and Δ ≈ 2.6 meV. The ratio Γ⁄Δ ≈ 14 % in our samples turns out to be
2-3 times larger than the values observed by tunneling spectroscopy on 1-2 µm thick Nb3Sn films for rf
applications [5,6] and Nb coupons [7,8]. The deviations of the STM data from the Dynes model may result
from local non-stoichiometry, scattering of quasiparticles on magnetic impurities, and a thin layer with
deteriorated superconducting properties at the surface [9,10]. The STEM data shown in Fig. 3 were obtained
in the group of Fumitake Kametani of Florida State University. The STM data shown in Fig. 4b were
obtained in the group of Lian Li of West Virginia State University. The SEM images revealed good texture
and crystallinity of the Nb3Sn layers and Al2O3 dielectric interlayers with relatively few short breaks, as
illustrated in Fig. 3. In turn, the STM data shown in Fig. 4b revealed sharp peaks in DOS at quasiparticle
energies close to the energy gap, and low density of subgap states.

 Growth of pnictide films and multilayers

The UW team has made many efforts to grow superconducting pnictide films and multilayers which offer
potential advantages over Nb3Sn. The UW postdoc Ki-Tae Eom has developed a pulse laser deposition
technique of growing Ba(Fe0.9 Co0.1)2As2 (Ba-122) ferropnictide films on strontium-titanite substrates. The

 5

UW group has developed a novel method of template engineering making use of single-crystal intermediate
layers of (001) SrTiO3 and BaTiO3 grown on various perovskite substrates to enable genuinely epitaxial
films of Co-doped BaFe2As2 with high transition temperature (Tc, = 21.5K), narrow transition widths (∆Tc
= 1.3K), and high critical current density Jc(4.2K) = 4.5 MA/cm2. The goal was to use the template
engineering to produce Co-doped BaFe2As2 films and multilayers of different thicknesses, a few cm wide
dielectric LaAlO3 and (La,Sr)(Al,Ta)O3 (LSAT) substrates suitable for the RF measurements.

While patterning these films, the UW team has encountered problems related to the adhesion to the substrate
and structural integrity and stoichiometry of the material. Eventually these problems had been overcome
and coplanar resonators like the one shown in the figure below were produced.

However, the subsequent RF tests on the RF probe station at CAS ODU and JLab have showed that these
resonators had poor RF properties. One reason was that superconducting properties of pnictides deteriorate
with time if the film is directly exposed to moisture in the air. To prevent that from happening, the UW
team deposited a platinum cap onto the pnictide film which preserved their superconducting properties but
produced unacceptably high eddy current losses under GHz field. The UW team then spent much time
developing dielectric capping films with low RF losses, but unfortunately, these efforts turned out to be
unsuccessful. It was found that all dielectric overlayers which can stick to the Ba(Fe0.9 Co0.1)2As2 (Ba-122)
ferropnictide films require deposition at high temperatures which, in turn, degrade superconducting
properties of pnictide films. As a result, all pnictide films capped with dielectric overlayers have shown no
superconducting response under RF field.

 THRUST 2: RF characterization of films and multilayers

RF tests of thin films and multilayers have been our most challenging problem because of no experimental
tools capable of producing RF fields of the order of 10-100 mT on the surface of flat samples without very
high parasitic losses caused by penetration of vortices from the edges. We started our measurements of the
surface resistance of thin film and multilayers using the surface impedance characterization (SIC) system
at JLab, under the agreement with C. Reece and A. Hutton. ODU graduate student Salvador Guirton worked
with Grigory Eremeev at JLab on these measurements. During the first two years we measured:
NbN/MgO/Nb multilayers grown by the W&M group, Nb/Cu and NbTiN films from Ann-Marie Valente-
Feliciano (JLab) and MgB2 films from ANL. The results turned out to be inconsistent with the previously
published data because of unreliable behavior of the SIC system which could not be fixed. Eventually, the
SIC system at JLab was decommissioned and we switched to other experimental setups.

Currently, a Quadrupole Resonator (QPR) is the only tool available to the SRF community to measure the
properties of flat superconducting samples of 50 to 75 mm in diameter at high RF fields and at frequencies
of interest for particle accelerators (from to ~ 0.2 GHz to ~1.5 GHz). Moreover, a QPR can measure the
same sample over a range of frequencies and temperatures, allowing us to address such outstanding SRF
issues as the fundamental limits of the Q factors and accelerating gradients, and to optimize the multilayer
technology - one of a prime goal of this project. Given that the QPR is the only system which can measure
flat samples, thin films and multilayers under the high RF field and low loss conditions most closely
matching those in the SRF cavities, our last two renewals of this project were assumed that our sample

 6

would be measured by the QPR system at JLab, as had been planned for many years. However, this hope
has not materialized because of delays with building a QPR system for JLab at CERN followed by Covid-
related delays and delays with setting up the QPR at JLab. As a result, our project ended before the QPR at
JLab became fully operational. In the meantime, we turned to other RF characterization techniques, such
as a pulse system at SLAC, RF probe station at CAS/JLab to characterize thin film stripline resonators and
a setup developed at CAS to measure dc breakdown fields on thin samples, as described below.

 a. Pulse measurements of quality factors of Nb3Sn multilayers

 Nb3Sn multilayers grown on 2” sapphire
 wafers were tested in a hemispherical Nb-
 coated cavity at SLAC National Accelerator
 Laboratory described in Ref. [11]. A rendering
 of this cavity is shown in Fig. 5bError!
 Reference source not found.. The cavity
 operates in a TE032-like mode at 11.4 GHz,
 with a pocket on the flat face for mounting 2”-
 diameter samples (shown in purple). The
 overall cavity quality factor is measured, and
 the properties of the wafer can be deduced by
 comparison with known samples. The
 geometry of the cavity is engineered such that
 the magnetic field is strongest at the sample
 surface, limiting the contribution of the cavity
 material to the overall cavity loss. According
 to simulations, the participation factor is 0.33
 for the 2”-diameter sample. Crucially, the
 magnetic field at the sample is in the radial
 direction and parallel to the sample surface,
 making it possible to measure RF properties of
 the sample without interference from the
 perpendicular component of the field.

 The SRF performance of two Nb3Sn samples
 were compared in this system to a cavity-grade
 bulk Nb coupon. A 500 nm (~4λ) Nb3Sn film
 intended to completely screen out the RF
 magnetic field, and a 3x60 nm Nb3Sn/Al2O3
 trilayer were tested under the RF field. Both
 samples were coated with a 200 nm Nb film on
  Fig. 5. (a) RF Surface resistance measurements of Nb3Sn film and
  multilayer compared to cavity-grade Nb. (b) Cutaway of
 the backside of the wafer to prevent leakage of
  hemispherical resonator cavity at SLAC used for these magnetic field as shown in Fig. 1. The quality
  measurements. factor of the cavity with each sample,
 measured at low power with a network
analyzer, is plotted in Fig. 5a. The abrupt increase in Q at about 15 K corresponds to the superconducting
transition of Nb3Sn, followed by an increase of Q(T) at Tc = 9K of the Nb-coated host cavity.

Fig. 5a shows that the thick Nb3Sn film and the trilayer have nearly identical Q at T < 9K, indicating that
Al2O3 dielectric layers and interfaces do not contribute significantly to the RF dissipation. We would expect
the thick Nb3Sn film to have a higher Q, as magnetic field is more fully screened before reaching the
substrate and backside, so this result suggests that the maximum Q of these films and multilayers is limited

 7

by the quality of the Nb3Sn material rather than by the interfaces with Al2O3. The quality factors of both
the film and the trilayer samples exceed Q(T) of Nb at T > 6K due to the higher Tc of Nb3Sn and is about 2
times smaller than Q of Nb at 4K.

Despite the non-stoichiometry and inhomogeneity of superconducting properties, grain boundaries, Nb
inclusions, and incomplete Al2O3 layers, our multilayers exhibit the quality factors on par of those of cavity-
grade bulk Nb at 4K and low RF power. The significant local non-stoichiometry of thick (a few micron)
polycrystalline Nb3Sn coatings of Nb cavities, as well as Sn depletion at grain boundaries in Nb3Sn have
been well documented in the literature [12-15]. Yet, with all these materials issues which are also
characteristic of 1-3 𝜇m thick Nb3Sn films used in SRF cavities, our Nb3Sn SIS structures exhibit higher
low-field Q values than Nb at T > 6K, consistent with the larger superconducting energy gap Δ#$%&' ≈
2Δ#$ and a lower BCS surface resistance 𝑅()* of Nb3Sn. Our results not only show a remarkable resilience
of low-field quality factors of Nb3Sn to the significant non-stoichiometry and materials imperfections but
also suggest that the SRF performance of Nb3Sn coatings can be further improved by materials treatments.
Our Nb3Sn multilayers exhibit a similar resilience of the low-power SRF performance to the materials
imperfections.

The slopes of Q(T) for both the Nb3Sn film and multilayer shown in Fig. 5 tend to level off at 4-5 K and
are clearly smaller than the slope of Q(T) for Nb. This indicates that Q(T) of the Nb3Sn samples at T = 4 -
5 K is not limited by the BCS surface resistance for which the slope of 𝑄(𝑇) ∝ exp(Δ⁄𝑘( 𝑇) for Nb3Sn
would be larger than for Nb because Δ#$%&' ≈ 2Δ#$ . The behavior of Q(T) of the Nb3Sn samples at 4-5 K
is thus indicative of a significant residual surface resistance caused by the multiphase structure of the films
and multilayers and trapped vortices. Yet 𝑄! ≃ 10+ observed on our Nb3Sn multilayers at 11.4 GHz and
4 K suggests values of 𝑄! ∼ 10, at 4K and 1 GHz given the frequency dependence Q ∝ 𝜔-" which comes
from the BCS surface resistance, ohmic losses in metallic precipitates smaller than the RF skin depth and
perhaps Josephson vortices trapped on grain boundaries [10,16].

SRF performance at high RF fields and breakdown fields of Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers are yet to be explored.
Generally, the effects of nonstoichiometry, proximity-coupled normal precipitates and weakly-coupled
grain boundaries become more pronounced at higher RF fields. For instance, nonstoichiometric grain
boundaries in Nb3Sn have been identified as prime pinning centers for vortices in Nb3Sn wires for high-
field dc magnets [12,13]. However, weakly-coupled grain boundaries in Nb3Sn coating layers would block
RF currents and cause dissipative penetration of Josephson vortices at fields well below the superheating
field [16], and sub-stoichiometric regions in Nb3Sn-coated Nb cavities are suspected to play an important
role in RF cavity quench [14,15]. At the same time, significant meandering and breaks in Al2O3 layers
shown in Figure 4 may not be detrimental for SRF performance as the layers can still provide their main
role of intercepting and pinning small vortex loops originating at surface structural defects 5,8 since the
pinholes sizes 10-50 nm in the Al2O3 layers are smaller than magnetic size of the vortex 𝜆 ≃ 100 − 200
nm of Nb3Sn. Though Al2O3 layers do not fully separate Nb3Sn layers, we found that a 500 nm thick Nb3Sn
film had a quality factor identical to a multilayer with three 60 nm Nb3Sn layers separated by 6 nm Al2O3,
and both had Q approximately 2x lower than a cavity-grade Nb reference. This is a strong evidence that the
ruggedness of Al2O3 and the oxide-metallic interfaces do not significantly contribute to the surface
resistance of samples prepared with this process.

In summary, a self-regulating, adsorption-controlled process developed at UW was used to grow Nb3Sn
films and Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers with up to four superconducting layers. Despite the detrimental effects
of nonstoichiometry, grain boundaries and breaks in the meandering Al2O3 interlayers, the SRF
performance of our multilayers turned out to be on par with that of Nb films. The growth technique
developed in this project provides a platform for further optimizations of the SRF properties of SIS high-
performance multilayers for superconducting resonator applications.

 8

 b. Measurements of the nonlinear Meissner effect in Nb3Sn coplanar waveguides

A low-field RF tests of Nb3Sn films grown at UW were performed by the ODU graduate student Junki
Makita on a cryogenic RF probe station which was set up at CAS for the measurements of RF properties of
thin films patterned in the form of meandering coplanar waveguides shown in Figs. 6 and 7. The work
described in this section was published in Ref. [17].

  Fig. 6. RF cryogenic probe station at CAS ODU
 Fig. 7. A patterned Nb3Sn meandering coplanar waveguide.

The probe station was equipped with a closed cycle cryocooler and a NbTi superconducting magnets
producing a dc up to 200 mT parallel to the film plane. This magnet was mounted on a six-motor hexapod
system that allowed for fine tuning of magnet field orientation by ±7. in three-axis while taking the sample
measurements, and the temperature of the sample was varied using a resistive heater underneath the sample
stage. The complex transmission coefficient S was measured as functions of temperature and the external
in-plane magnetic field B. The resonant frequencies and the field-dependent London penetration depth 𝜆(𝐵)
were extracted by fitting the dependence of the phase on frequency of the transmission spectra. To minimize
the number of trapped vortices during cooldown of the sample through Tc, we used three pairs of Helmholtz
coils to reduce the ambient field. The field was measured by a magnetometer while adjusting the coil
currents to achieve less than 4 mG at optimal current combination. The results for a coplanar waveguide
made of a 50 nm Nb3Sn film deposited onto a 10x10 mm sapphire substrate are described below.

We first attempted to explain the above results by the conventional mechanism of frequency shift due to
reduction of the superfluid density by the Meissner current pairbreaking [18-21]. We did so by calculating
𝛿𝑓⁄𝑓! by solving the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau equation at 𝐿/ ≫ 𝐿0 and obtained [17]:

where 𝜑 is the angle between the applied dc magnetic field and the waveguide, 𝜔 is the RF frequency, and
𝜏 is the energy relaxation time constant of quasiparticles on phonons, 𝜔𝜏 ≪ 1 at GHz frequencies and low
temperatures. Eq. (2) cannot explain our data shown in Fig. 10 because: 1. It predicts a quadratic field
dependence of 𝛿𝑓⁄𝑓! instead of the observe quasi-linear dependence, 2. The shift 𝛿𝑓⁄𝑓! is larger for B
perpendicular the waveguide (𝜑 = 90. ) than for B parallel to the waveguide (𝜑 = 0. ), whereas the

 10

experiment shows the opposite, 3. Even in the extreme limit of 𝐿/ ≫ 𝐿0 the frequency shift given by Eq.
(2) is smaller than the observed one.

Based on the above results, we have concluded that the field and orientational dependencies of 𝜆(𝐵) are
controlled much stronger mechanisms than the conventional Meissner pairbreaking. We have identified the
polycrystallinity of our films as a leading reason for the anomalous behavior of 𝛿𝑓 ⁄𝑓! shown in Fig. 10. A
new mean-field model was developed in which the features of 𝛿𝑓⁄𝑓! are explained by large kinetic
inductances of the Josephson array of weakly coupled grain boundaries. The frequency shifts 𝛿𝑓⁄𝑓!
calculated in this model are shown in Fig. 11.

Given that our model does capture the key features of the experimental data, lead us to the following
conclusions: 1. Grain boundaries in Nb3Sn are essential SRF performance-limiting mechanisms in thin
films and most likely in Nb3Sn coatings of Nb cavities. 2. Grain boundaries can significantly enhance the
field dependence of the magnetic penetration depth, resulting in an anomalous nonlinear Meissner effect.

 c. Measurements of dc breakdown and superheating fields on multilayers

 Measurements of the breakdown field Hb in multilayers
 which exceed the superheating fields HsNb of the cavity-
 grade Nb under strong RF fields is a difficult task. We
 suggested and implemented a new approach, in which
 the breakdown fields of multilayers are measured under
 dc conditions. In this setup a small superconducting
 coil is put at a distance Lc from the film and the current
 in the coil is increased. Above a threshold current in
 the coil the Meissner currents density induced along a
 ring of radius ~ Lc over the film surface reaches the
 Fig. 12: Schematics of the experimental setup to measure depairing limit, leading to penetration of magnetic flux
 a dc breakdown magnetic field in film and multilayers. through the film. This event is detected by Hall probes
 on the backside of the substrate registering the onset of
flux penetration as the maximum parallel field produced by the magnet at the field surface Hmax reaches Hs,
whereas at Hmax < Hs the Hall probe detects no signal, as shown in Fig. 12. In turn, Hb can be calculated
for a given geometry of the magnet using standard EM software packages.

This setup has been bult and thoroughly calibrated and tested by the ODU graduate student Harshani
Senevirathne. The actual system depicted in Fig. 13 is now operational at JLab, where it has been used for

 11

the measurements of breakdown fields on thin superconducting films and multilayers grown both in UW,
ODU (by the group of Hani Elsaed-Ali at ECE) and at JLab (by the group of Anne-marie Valente-
Feliciano). Among advantages of this method are its simplicity and the ability to apply dc fields close to
the superheating fields of tested materials, mimicking the breakdown of SRF cavities. The results described
in this section were published in Ref. [22].

 THRUST 3. Theory development

Theoretical work in this project has advanced in different directions addressing outstanding fundamental
issues of SRF physics of prime importance for particle accelerators. They include: 1. Mechanisms of
nonlinear BCS surface resistance and its dependence on the RF field and frequency. 2. Mechanisms of the
negative Q(H) slope observed on SRF cavities. 3. The limits of SRF breakdown field and the physics of
dynamic superheating field. 4. Enhancement of the field breakdown limits by surface nano-structuring and
multilayer coating. 4. Nonlinear losses caused by penetration of vortices along grain boundaries, 6.

 13

 Nonlinear losses caused by trapped vortices driven by strong RF field. Brief descriptions of the progress
 in each direction are given below, relegating technical details to our referred publications.

 1. Nonlinear BCS resistance and negative Q(H) slope

 A theory of the negative Q(H) slope and microwave
 suppression of Rs(H) observed on N and O doped Nb cavities,
 was developed by PI [25]. In this theory nonlinear time-
 dependent Usadel equations of nonequilibrium
 superconductivity were solved to calculate the nonlinear
 surface resistance, which generalizes the Mattis-Bardeen theory
 [26,27] to strong rf fields H ~ Hc in the dirty limit. It was shown
 that the reduction of Rs(H) with the RF field comes from the
 well-known effect of current-induced broadening of the gap
 peaks in the quasiparticle density of states [19,28,29]. Essential
 mechanisms contributing to the extended Q(H) rise, particularly
 nonequilibrium kinetics of quasiparticles [27] and subgap states
 in the quasiparticle density of states (DOS) were pointed out.
 Fig. 17. Fit of the theory [25] to the first
 experiment on Ti-doped Nb cavities [30].
 The  theory predicted two-three-fold reduction of Rs(H) at 40-
 150 mT relative to Rs(0) for Nb, capturing the feature of
 negative Q-slope in Ti, N or O-doped cavities observed by
 different groups [30-38]. A theory fit for a Ti-doped cavity measured at JLab [30] is shown in Fig. 17.

 In collaboration with the visiting scholar Takayuki Kubo, the theory of PI was generalized to incorporate
 residual resistance Ri caused by subgap quasiparticle states in the bulk DOS and an imperfect surface
 covered by a thin proximity coupled non-superconducting layer which models a suboxide structure of Nb
 surface. We have shown that Ri can be affected by the properties of the normal layer which can account for
 the significant effect of materials treatment such as low-temperature baking on the residual resistance. This
 was a part of a theory project on new ways of reducing losses of SRF cavities using pairbeaking mechanisms
 detrimental to superconductivity at zero frequency. Our results published in Ref. [9,10,39] have shown
 that, contrary to the common belief, these mechanisms can be used to reduce SRF losses by engineering an
 optimum density of states beneficial to the surface resistance. Examples are shown in Figs. 18-20.

 14

Fig. 18 shows that the subgap state result in the temperature-independent residual surface resistance at low
temperatures. Our theory gave the following formula for the residual resistance [9,21]:

 𝜇!" 𝜔" 𝜆% Γ "

According to Eq. (3), 𝑅1 = 4 𝑛Ω for clean Nb at 1.5 GHz is achieved at Γ = 0.03Δ, These Γ values are
consistent with those observed by STM imaging of nitrogen doped Nb cavities [40]. Fig. 18 shows that,
while 𝑅1 increases with Γ in agreement with Eq. (3), 𝑅3 (𝑇) at higher temperatures decreases as Γ is
increased. The latter is due to broadening the gap peaks in the DOS, which allows using pairbreaking
mechanisms to reduce SRF losses. For instance, Fig. 19 shows how Rs is reduced by doping Nb with a
small concentration of magnetic impurities, even though they decrease Tc. We also showed that the surface
resistance can be reduced by optimizing the interface resistance between the normal suboxide layer and
bulk Nb, as shown in Fig. 20. Our theory was used to explain STM experiments on Nb coupons cut off
nitrogen-doped cavities and show that the N-doping primarily shrinks the normal suboxide layer [40].

We further developed the above theory to include the effect of subgap states and a normal suboxide layer
on the RF field dependence of the surface resistance [39]. This theory revealed complex interplay of the
Dynes parameter, thickness of the suboxide layer and the resistive barrier between the suboxide layer and
the bulk Nb. Some representative results of our extensive calculations are shown below:

representative results as shown in the figures below.

1. We have developed a theory of RF dissipation caused by Josephson vortices driven along weakly-
 coupled grain boundaries by strong currents in multilayers and granular films (see Fig. 3) and performed
 extensive numerical simulations of the RF power caused by nonlinear dynamics of Josephson vortices.

The results shown in Figs 20 and 21 reveal complex effects of thickness of the suboxide layer and the
interface resistance on the field dependence of surface resistance, negative Q-slope SRF losses. Because
the parameters of suboxide layer can be significantly altered by the materials treatments, the theory can
explain the great variability of the cavity Q factors and guide for optimization of SRF losses.

Along with the postdoc Ahmad Sheikhzada, we had started calculations of the field-dependent surface
resistance affected by nonequilibrium kinetics of quasiparticles driven by strong RF field in SRF cavities.
These calculations involve numerical simulations of nonlinear equations of nonequilibrium
superconductivity augmented by integro-differential kinetic equations describing energy relaxation of
quasiparticles by inelastic scattering on phonons [27]. These calculations turned out to be quite involved
and have not been completed by the end of the project.

 2. Calculations of breakdown field limits and dynamic superheating field

The postdoc Ahmad Sheikhzada and PI have calculated breakdown field limits under GHz RF field. The
ultimate field limit in the SRF community is usually associated with the static superheating field Hs at which
the Meissner state becomes unstable and a transition to a dissipative state occurs. Currently Hs has been
primarily calculated using the Ginzburg-Landau (GL) equations applicable at T close to Tc. It was found
that that 𝐻3 (𝜅) decreases monotonically with the GL parameter 𝜅 = 𝜆⁄𝜉 from 𝐻3 ≈ 1.2𝐻4 at 𝜅 = 1 to
𝐻3 = 0.745𝐻4 at 𝜅 ≫ 1, where 𝐻4 is the thermodynamic critical magnetic field. The superheating field for
cavity applications at 𝑇 ≪ 𝑇4 has been usually evaluated by extrapolating the GL results to low
temperatures, where the GL theory is invalid. Microscopic calculations of 𝐻3 (𝑇) in the entire range 0 <
𝑇 < 𝑇4 have only been done for 𝜅 → ∞ in which 𝐻3 = 0.84𝐻4 at T = 0 exceeds the GL extrapolation
[41,42]. The effect of nonmagnetic and magnetic impurities on Hs(T) calculated by solving the Eilenberger
equations at 𝜅 ≫ 1 and 0 < T < Tc was addressed in our earlier work [43].

An important question is whether the dc superheating field is the true field limit of the Meissner state under
strong GHz field. The answer depends on the relation between the time formation of the vortex core tv and
the rf period: if 𝑡5 𝜔 ≫ 1 vortices do not have enough time to form and the dynamic superheating field Hd
may exceed the static Hs. The core formation time 𝑡5 can be evaluated as 𝑡5 ∼ 𝜉 ⁄𝑣6 , where vd is the
terminal velocity of a vortex penetrating through the surface at H = Hs [9]. Measurements of vd in Pb [44]
and Nb-C [45] gave 𝑣6 ∼ 10 − 20 km/s. For Nb, we obtained 𝑡5 𝜔 ∼ 10-" − 10-% at GHz frequencies for
which penetration of vortices occurs nearly instantaneously once H(t) exceeds Hs. Calculation of a dynamic

superheating field 𝐻6 (𝑇, 𝜔) or a dynamic depairing current density 𝑗6 (𝑇, 𝜔) = 𝐻6 ⁄𝜆 is a challenging
problem which requires numerically solving equations of nonequilibrium superconductivity which account

 16

for the effects of rf current pairbreaking on the DOS and Δ(𝑡) and the energy relaxation due to inelastic
scattering of quasiparticles on phonons [27,46]. This complicated computational task was solved by Ahmad
Sheikhzada and PI in Ref. [47]. The results are shown in Fig. 23. The key result is that the dynamic
superheating field can exceed the static Hs by up to 40% at frequencies 𝜔𝜏7 > 1, where 𝜏7 is the energy
relaxation time of quasiparticles on phonons [27,48].

 3. Enhancement of the breakdown field by surface nano-structuring and multilayer coating.

Following the original SIS multilayer idea [49], PI has calculated an optimal multilayer thickness d = dc at
which the SIS structure can screen the maximum field Hm in the Meissner state [50]:

 Here the S layers with a bulk superheating field Hs and the

 superheating field Hs0 and the penetration depth l0, where µ

multilayers as shown in Fig. 24. Here N or O infusion of the Nb layers would produce the extended Q(H)
observed on bulk Nb cavities but without the reduced breakdown field.

The reduction of Hb in the N-doped cavities may result from the formation of a few µm thick dirty layer at
the surface with reduced l and increased GL parameter k = l/x » l0/l, where x » (lx0)1/2 is the dirty-limit
coherence length. Such thick dirty layer reduces both the bulk superheating field Hs and the lower critical
field Hc1 = (f0/4pl2)(lnk + 0.5), where f0 = 2×10-15 Wb is the magnetic flux quantum. For instance, the GL
theory predicts that Hs at T » Tc decreases from Hs » 1.2Hc at k » 1 (clean Nb) to Hs » 0.745Hc at Tc in the
limit of k >>1 (dirty Nb), where Hc is the thermodynamic critical field [10]. In turn, the use of SIS optimized
N-doped Nb multilayers enables one to reverse the reduction of Hb in N or O-doped Nb cavities and increase
Hb up to Hm ® (Hs2 + Hs02)1/2 which can exceed 200 mT by as much as 50% at k >> 1 and T << Tc where
Hs ® 0.84Hc [41-43]. This approach offers an opportunity to fully reveal the extended Q(H) rise of nitrogen
or oxygen-infused Nb cavities to the RF fields exceeding 100 mT.

Given the limitations of the London model for the calculations of the superheating field, the graduate
student Manula Pathirana and PI have calculated Hs by solving the GL equations. The main computation
challenge here was to account that at a finite GL parameter, the superconducting state at the surface becomes
unstable with respect to a periodic perturbation with a wavelength 𝐿4 ≃ (𝜆𝜉 % ):/< once the ramping field
H(t) reaches Hs. We have calculated Hs for a dirty layer at the surface and a SIS structure. The dirty layer
was modeled by a gradually reduced coherence length at the surface 𝜉 " (𝑥)⁄𝜉!" = 1 − 𝛼exp (− 𝑥⁄𝑙6 ),

 17

where 𝛼 quantifies the level of enhanced concentration of impurities in a layer of width 𝑙6 at the surface.
The results of our calculations are shown in the Fig. 25.

The left figures show that a dirty layer at the surface can increase the superheating field by as much as 10-
20%. The right figure shows that a Nb3Sn thin layer deposited onto a bulk Nb can boost the superheating
field to as high as 480 mT at an optimum thickness of the overlayer 𝑑8 ≃ 160 nm. Here the maximum in
𝐻3 (𝑑) and 𝐻3 (𝑙6 ) results from a current counterflow induced by the bulk Nb in the surface layer [50].

 4. Limitations imposed by grain boundaries on SRF performance of thin multilayers.

The enhancement of the magnetic breakdown field by thin film multilayer coating may be hampered by
weakly coupled grain boundaries characteristic of such promising SRF material as Nb3Sn [12-15]. For this
reason, the graduate student Ahmad Sheikhzada and PI have calculated dynamics of penetration of vortices
along GBs in thin films under RF field and losses such vortices can produce. The results were published in
Refs. [53-55]. The main issue is that the Josephson critical current densities through the GBs in high-
performance Nb3Sn in SRF cavities is large enough 𝐽4 > 𝐽6 ⁄𝜅 with 𝜅 ≃ 20 − 30, in which case the vortex
moving along GB turns into a mixed Abrikosov vortex with a Josephson core. Numerical simulations of
static and dynamic behaviors of such AJ vortices described by integro-differential equations of nonlocal
Josephson electrodynamics [56,57] were performed by Ahmad Sheikhzada. His simulations have shown
that the AJ vortices on GBs have novel features as compared to the conventional Josephson vortices in low-
Jc grain boundaries. 1. AJ vortices moving along underdamped GBs emit Cherenkov radiation which
becomes the main source of vortex drag and dissipation at high driving currents. 2. As a RF current density
exceeds a threshold value 𝐽3 < 𝐽4 below the critical density 𝐽4 of the Josephson junction, the Cherenkov
trailing wake causes splitting of a moving vortex and continuous production of vortex-anivortex pairs,
accompanied by explosive increase of power dissipation. Shown in Fig. 26 is the steady-state velocity if
the vortex as a function of the dc driving current 𝛽 = 𝐽⁄𝐽4 calculated for GBs with different dimensionless
damping parameters 𝜂 proportional to the Ohmic conductance of the boundary. All velocity curves 𝑣(𝛽)
have end points beyond which the vortex can no longer move with a constant velocity and stats producing

We extended the above results for a single GB to the 2d array of Josephson junctions modeling a
polycrystalline film. This work was done in collaboration with Francisco Esteles-Duart, Miguel Ortuno and
Andreas Somosa of University of Murcia, Spain and Valerii Vinokur of ANL [60]. The results of our
numerical simulations of current-driven vortices in the 2D Josephson junction arrays are shown below.

 Fig. 29. Top: geometry of the 2d Josephson Fig. 30: Color map of magnetic field showing: Linear proliferation of
 junction array. Bottom: Cherenkov cone V-AV pairs (top) and dendritic branching of V-AV chains (bottom).
 behind a moving vortex. Vortices and antivortices are shown by red and blue, respectively.

We observed a new mechanism of production of vortex-antivortex pairs propagating in Josephson junction
array remarkably resembling dendritic crack propagation during mechanical fracture of solids [61].

We extended our theory for vortices in long single Josephson junctions and large Josephson junction arrays
to single GBs in thin films and multilayers and published the results in Refs. [55,62,63]. For instance, we
showed that vortices trapped in a GB in a film bounce back and forth under the dc current. Such vortex
shuttle gets unstable at 𝐽 > 𝐽3 and generates V-AV pairs which also bounce back and forth and get
synchronized, resulting in switching the entire GB into a dissipative resistive state. For a vortex trapped in
a multilayer, this process causes generation of resonant cavity modes in a stack of coupled layers, giving
rise to stimulated THz emission from the multilayer [62,63]. The dependence of vortex velocity on driving
current in a multilayer turned out to be similar to that in a single Josephson junction. Shown below are the
𝑣(𝛽) curves along with the threshold current density 𝐽3 (𝜂) as a function of the damping parameter,
calculated for a multilayer in Ref. [63].

 Fig. 31. (a) Velocity of a vortex as a function of current in a stack of 21 coupled superconducting
 layers. (b) Threshold current of V-AV pair production as a function of damping parameter [63].

 5. Nonlinear RF losses caused by trapped vortices in SRF cavities

Trapped vortices in SRF cavities are one of the main sources of the residual surface resistance 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔)
[10,21] which depends of rf frequency and the RF field amplitude quite differently from the nonlinear BCS
surface resistance discussed in the previous sections. As a result, separation of the BCS and residual surface
resistances at high rf fields from the measured Q(H) curves is a nontrivial procedure which generally
requires knowledge of underlying physical mechanisms behind 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔). To unravel the features of
residual resistance caused by trapped vortices, the graduate student Manula Pathirana and PI have
performed numerical simulations of a trapped elastic vortex driven by a strong ac magnetic field 𝐻(𝑡) =
𝐻 sin 𝜔𝑡 parallel to the surface of a superconducting. The surface resistance and the power dissipated by
an oscillating elastic vortex perpendicular to the film surface was calculated as functions of H and 𝜔 for
different spatial distributions, densities and strengths of pinning centers, including bulk pinning, surface
pinning and cluster pinning shown in Fig. 32. We have shown that the local surface resistance 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔, 𝒓)
exhibits strong mesoscopic fluctuations due to different initial lateral positions of the vortex in a random
potential, and a sequence of local depinning jumps of a vortex segment as the field amplitude increases.
Yet the global surface resistance 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) obtained by averaging of local 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔, 𝒓) over realizations of
random pinning potential exhibits a nearly linear increase with the field amplitude a small H and levels of
at higher fields. Our simulations were performed for both the Bardeen-Stephen viscous vortex drag
applicable at low fields and a nonlinear Larkin-Ovchinnikov viscous drag coefficient 𝜂(𝑣) at high fields.
We have shown that the decrease of 𝜂(𝑣) with the vortex velocity 𝑣 can produce a nonmonotonic field
dependence of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) which decreases with H at higher fields, thus giving one more possible mechanism
of the negative Q(H) slope unrelated to the BCS quasiparticle surface resistance. Our simulations have also
revealed a plethora of nonlinear effects such as jumpwise instabilities of a curvilinear pinned vortex. We
also considered overheating of RF-driven vortices which can mask the descending field dependence of
𝑅1 (𝐻) as the frequency increases. These results were published in Refs. [64,65].

pinning vortices. Here 𝑅! = 𝜌2 𝐵! ⁄𝜆𝐵4" , 𝑓! = 𝐵4: 𝜌2 ⁄𝐵4" 𝜆" 𝜇! , and 𝐵! is the flux density of trapped
vortices. For a moderately dirty Nb with 𝜅 = 4, an estimate gave 𝑓! ≃ 22 GHz [65]. Three essential
conclusions can be drawn from our results shown in Fig. 34: 1. 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) increases rapidly with 𝜔 in the
frequency range which widens as the pinning strength increases. 2. Increasing the applied RF field does not
substantially alter the frequency dependence of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔). 3. The sublinear dependence of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) on 𝜔
implies that contribution of trapped flux to RF losses becomes more dominant as lower frequency at which
𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) exceeds the BCS surface resistance 𝑅()* (𝜔, 𝐻) ∝ 𝜔" which decreases rapidly as 𝜔 is decreased.
The latter implies that trapped flux for which 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) increases with H (see Fig. 33) can mask the
anomalous 𝑄(𝐻) rise coming from 𝑅()* (𝜔, 𝐻) ∝ 𝜔" as the frequency decreases. This conclusion is
consistent with experiment [67].

 22

Fig. 35. Residual resistance taking into account the LO velocity-dependent vortex drag calculated for Nb at 𝜅 = 2, pin strength
𝜁' = 0.8, pin density 𝑛- = 0.25𝜆./ and frequencies: (a) 𝛾 = 0.01, (b) 𝛾 = 0.4 , (c) 𝛾 = 1. Here the parameter 𝛼 = (𝜆𝑓⁄𝑣# )1
quantifies the strength of the LO effect [65].

Our calculations revealed new features of field dependence of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) resulting from the LO decrease of
vortex drag coefficient 𝜂(𝑣) = 𝜂! ⁄[1 + (𝑣⁄𝑣! )" ] with velocity. We found than this decrease mitigates the
RF vortex losses at higher fields, giving rise to 𝑄(𝐻) rise coming from trapped magnetic flux. Examples of
𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) calculated with the LO velocity-dependent vortex drag are shown in Fig. 35. Here the descending
field dependence of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) at low frequencies turns into a nonmonotonic one as the frequency increases.
Because of variability of superconducting parameters and impurity concentration at the surface of SRF
cavities, the dimensionless frequency 𝛾 = 𝑓 ⁄𝑓! normalized to 𝑓! = 𝐻4: 𝜌2 ⁄𝐻4" 𝜆" 𝜇! can vary substantially
depending on cavity treatments.

We calculated 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) taking into account overheating caused by oscillating vortex lines by solving Eqs.
(5) and (6) coupled with a temperature diffusion equation. It was shown that overheating causes an
additional increase of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) with the RF field as compared to the isothermal 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔) shown in Fig. 33
and 35. As the same time, overheating can cause thermal breakdown of a cavity at Rf fields below the dc
superheating field, as it has been shown in Refs. [68-70]. Overall, the residual resistance caused by trapped
vortices can show a large variety of different field and frequency dependencies, reflecting the complexity
of underlying physics of vortices driven with supersonic velocities by strong RF fields in SRF cavities [71].
Our results give a computational framework to address a plethora of such different behaviors of 𝑅1 (𝐻, 𝜔)
and suggest ways of mitigating the SRF losses caused by trapped magnetic flux. Reducing RF losses caused
by trapped flux can be achieved by pushing vortex hotspots out of cavities by temperature gradients
produced by external heaters [72,73] or scanning laser beams [69], as we have proposed earlier.

                                       PHYSICAL REVIEW B 102, 104507 (2020)

 Dynamic pair-breaking current, critical superfluid velocity, and nonlinear
 electromagnetic response of nonequilibrium superconductors
 *
 Ahmad Sheikhzada and Alex Gurevich†
 Department of Physics and Center for Accelerator Science, Old Dominion University, Norfolk, Virginia 23529, USA

 (Received 20 April 2020; revised 19 June 2020; accepted 20 August 2020; published 15 September 2020)

 We report numerical calculations of a dynamic pair-breaking current density Jd and a critical superfluid
 velocity vd in a nonequilibrium superconductor carrying a uniform, large-amplitude AC current density J (t ) =
 Ja sin t with  well below the gap frequency   0 /h̄. The dependencies Jd (, T ) and vd (, T ) near the
 critical temperature Tc were calculated from either the full time-dependent nonequilibrium equations for a dirty
 s-wave superconductor or the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations for a gapped superconductor,
 taking into account the GL relaxation time of the order parameter τGL and the inelastic electron-phonon relaxation
 time of quasiparticles τE . We show that both approaches give similar frequency dependencies of J√ d () and
 v√d () which gradually increase from their static pair-breaking GL values Jc and vc at τE  1 to 2Jc and
 2vc at τE  1. Here Jd , vd and a dynamic superheating field at which the Meissner state becomes unstable
 were calculated in two different regimes of a fixed AC current and a fixed AC superfluid velocity induced by
 the applied AC magnetic field H = Ha sin t in a thin superconducting filament or a type-II superconductor
 with a large GL parameter. We also calculated a nonlinear electromagnetic response of a nonequilibrium
 superconducting state, particularly a dynamic kinetic inductance and a dissipative quasiparticle conductivity,
 taking into account the oscillatory dynamics of superconducting condensate and the kinetics of quasiparticles
 driven by a strong AC current. It is shown that an AC current density produces multiple harmonics of the electric
 field, the amplitudes of the higher-order harmonics diminishing as τE increases.

 104507-15

                                      PHYSICAL REVIEW B 101, 064504 (2020)

 Nonlinear dynamics and dissipation of a curvilinear vortex driven by a
 strong time-dependent Meissner current
 W. P. M. R. Pathirana and A. Gurevich
 Department of Physics and Center for Accelerator Science, Old Dominion University, Norfolk, Virginia 23529, USA

 (Received 12 November 2019; revised manuscript received 21 January 2020; accepted 23 January 2020;
 published 5 February 2020)

 We report numerical simulations of large-amplitude oscillations of a trapped vortex line under a strong ac
 magnetic field H (t ) = H sin ωt parallel to the surface. The power dissipated by an oscillating vortex segment
 driven by the surface ac Meissner currents was calculated by taking into account the nonlinear vortex line tension,
 vortex mass, and a nonlinear Larkin-Ovchinnikov (LO) viscous drag coefficient η(v). We show that the LO
 decrease of η(v) with the vortex velocity v can radically change the field dependence of the surface resistance
 Ri (H ) caused by trapped vortices. At low frequencies, Ri (H ) exhibits a conventional increase with H , but as ω
 increases, the surface resistance becomes a nonmonotonic function of H which decreases with H at higher fields.
 The effects of frequency, pin spacing, and the mean-free path li on the field dependence of Ri (H ) were calculated.
 It is shown that, as the surface gets dirtier and li decreases, the anomalous drop of Ri (H ) with H shifts to lower
 fields which can be much smaller than the lower critical magnetic field. Our numerical simulations also show that
 the LO decrease of η(v) with v can cause a vortex bending instability at high field amplitudes and frequencies,
 giving rise to the formation of dynamic kinks along the vortex. Measurements of Ri (H ) caused by sparse vortices
 trapped perpendicular to the surface can offer opportunities to investigate an extreme nonlinear dynamics of
 vortices driven by strong current densities up to the depairing limit at low temperatures. The behavior of Ri (H ),
 which can be tuned by varying the rf frequency or concentration of nonmagnetic impurities, is not masked by
 strong heating effects characteristic of dc or pulse transport measurements.

              DOI: 10.1103/PhysRevB.101.064504

 Figure 8 shows ri (β ) calculated for different values of
 frequency γ and the mean-free path li . At the lowest frequency
 γ0 = 0.004 the curves ri (β ) shown in Fig. 8(a) exhibit the
 monotonic decrease with β similar to that discussed in Sec. III
 in the low-γ limit of the ripple length Lω exceeding the pin
 distance l. As the frequency increases, a nonmonotonic field
 dependence of ri (β ) develops, the peaks in ri (β ) shifting to
 lower fields as the ratio li /ξ0 decreases. Notice the jumps
 preceding the peaks in ri (β ) in the case of γ0 = 0.04 shown
 in Fig. 8(b). The nonmonotonic dependence of ri (β ) is a
 manifestation of the transition from the case of Lω < l at low
 fields to Lω > l at higher fields, as was discussed above. In the
 case of γ0 = 0.4 represented by Fig. 8(c), the nonmonotonic

 V. DYNAMIC KINKS ALONG A VORTEX
 The vortex line tension suppresses the jumpwise LO in-
 stability which nevertheless manifests itself in the anomalous
 decrease of the surface resistance ri (β ) with the rf field am-
 plitude at β > β p . Yet, a principal question remains whether
 there is a range of the parameters in which the nonmonotonic
 LO velocity dependence of the drag force could cause a
 dynamic shape instability of a moving vortex. Indeed, once
 the velocity of a small vortex segment exceeds v0 , the local
 vortex drag diminishes further increasing v(x, t ) and resulting
 in growing shear stress between the fast vortex tip at x  λ and
 a slower part of the vortex at x  λ. In this section, we show
 that at large enough frequencies dynamic solutions of Eq. (4)
 become singular, formally indicating a vortex teardown as
 the restoring effect of vortex line tension cannot counter the
 dynamic LO shear stress at strong rf drives β > βc (γ , l ).

ferent values of li /ξ0 and l/λ0 = 3.

 where λJ = s , s is the interlayer spacing, 0 =
 φ02 /4π μ0 λ2 , and logarithmic factors ∼1 were disregarded.
 The LO instability of a stack of pancake vortices at high
 frequencies is considered in Appendix B. In this case, the
 Lorentz force driving slow pancake vortices (v < v0 ) in the
 stack is mostly countered by the viscous drag force, resulting
 in small amplitudes of oscillations yn  ymax ≃ v0 / f for each
 2D vortex. For v0 = 100 m/s and f = 10 GHz, the max-
 imum amplitude ymax ≃ 10 nm is much smaller than λ =
 150–400 nm in cuprates at T = 0. However, once the velocity
 of the pancake vortex at the surface v ∼ Hφ0 /λη0 exceeds
 v0 , it accelerates rapidly so that the magnetic and Joseph-
 son restoring forces may stop the runaway vortex at larger
 distances y0 ∼ min(λ, λJ ). Thus, the amplitudes of pancake
 vortices at the surface increase greatly, resulting in a dynamic
 kink along the 2D vortex stack.

 www.nature.com/scientificreports

 OPEN Development and characterization
 of ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 superconducting
 multilayers for particle accelerators
 Chris Sundahl1, Junki Makita2, Paul B. Welander3, Yi‑Feng Su4, Fumitake Kametani4,5,
 Lin Xie6, Huimin Zhang7, Lian Li7, Alex Gurevich2* & Chang‑Beom Eom1*

 Superconducting radio-frequency (SRF) resonator cavities provide extremely high quality factors > ­1010
 at 1–2 GHz and 2 K in large linear accelerators of high-energy particles. The maximum accelerating
 field of SRF cavities is limited by penetration of vortices into the superconductor. Present state-of-the-
 art Nb cavities can withstand up to 50 MV/m accelerating gradients and magnetic fields of 200–240
 mT which destroy the low-dissipative Meissner state. Achieving higher accelerating gradients requires
 superconductors with higher thermodynamic critical fields, of which ­Nb3Sn has emerged as a leading
 material for the next generation accelerators. To overcome the problem of low vortex penetration
 field in ­Nb3Sn, it has been proposed to coat Nb cavities with thin film ­Nb3Sn multilayers with dielectric
 interlayers. Here, we report the growth and multi-technique characterization of stoichiometric
 ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers with good superconducting and RF properties. We developed an adsorption-
 controlled growth process by co-sputtering Nb and Sn at high temperatures with a high overpressure
 of Sn. The cross-sectional scanning electron transmission microscope images show no interdiffusion
 between ­Al2O3 and ­Nb3Sn. Low-field RF measurements suggest that our multilayers have quality
 factor comparable with cavity-grade Nb at 4.2 K. These results provide a materials platform for
 the development and optimization of high-performance SIS multilayers which could overcome the
 intrinsic limits of the Nb cavity technology.

 For decades, Nb has been the material of choice for the radio-frequency superconducting (SRF) resonators for
 high-energy particle accelerators. Technological advances have resulted in the development of Nb cavities which
 can exhibit extremely high quality factors Q > ­1010 @ 1–2 GHz and 2 K while sustaining accelerating gradients
 up to 50 MV/m1–3. Such exemplary performance and low RF losses can only be achieved if the cavities operate
 in a Meissner state which can persist up to the maximum magnetic field at the inner cavity surface reaches the
 superheating field ­Bs = 240 ­mT1–3. At B = ­Bs the low-dissipative Meissner state becomes absolutely unstable with
 respect to dissipative penetration of vortices, causing an explosive increase of RF power and thermal quench of
 the cavity. The state-of-the-art Nb cavities can already operate at the peak magnetic fi ld close to B  ­ s, thus, increas-
 ing accelerating gradients beyond the intrinsic limits of Nb requires materials with higher ­Bs. There are many such
 materials but all of them are type-II superconductors with lower critical field B ­ c1 smaller than Bc1 ≈ 170−180
 mT of Nb which makes high-Bs superconductors prone to detrimental penetration of vortices at low f­ i lds4,5.
 To overcome this problem, it was proposed to nanostructure the inner surface of Nb cavities by coating it with
 multilayers of thin superconductors (S) separated by dielectric insulating (I) layers (Fig. 1)6. Here the S-layer
 material has a superheating field B­ s higher than B ­ s0 of Nb, whereas the thickness d of S layers is smaller than the
 London penetration depth , and the thickness of I layers can be a few nm to suppress the interlayer Josephson
 coupling. Such SIS structures greatly increase barriers for penetration of vortices in the bulk of the cavity which
 could potentially withstand the RF fields limited by the superheating fi ld of S-layer. For instance, using N ­ b3Sn
 with Bs = 480 mT could nearly double the maximum accelerating gradient as compared to the best Nb cavities.
 The multilayer approach is based on the lack of thermodynamically stable parallel vortices in thin decoupled S

 West Virginia University, Morgantown, WV 26506, USA. *email: agurevic@odu.edu; eom@engr.wisc.edu

Scientific Reports |   (2021) 11:7770                  | https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-021-87119-9                                                        1

 Figure 1.  Schematic of ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayer heterostructures on ­Al2O3 wafer. Back side of ­Al2O3 wafer is
 coated with a thick Nb film.

 screens at B < Bc1 where Bc1 is strongly enhanced in films with d < 6–10. Because the inner surface of the Nb
 cavity is partially screened by multilayers, both Q(H) and the breakdown field can be increased due to lower
 surface resistance R­ s and higher H ­ c of the layer ­material6.
 The multilayer coating, which opens up a principal opportunity to break the Nb monopoly in SRF cavities,
 has been tested by several groups using ­MgB2, ­Nb3Sn, NbN, NbTiN, and dirty Nb as coating materials. These
 experiments have shown an increase of the dc field onset of penetration of vortices on Nb surfaces coated with
 different SIS ­structures4,11–19, although such key SRF characteristics as the surface resistance and quality factors
 of SIS multilayers under high-amplitude RF fields have been investigated to a much lesser extent. The first results
 on low-field Q measurements on NbN/MgO m ­ ultilayers13,19 have shown that SIS multilayers can have lower R
 ­s
 than bulk Nb. However, the SRF performance of ­Nb3Sn, the current material of choice for the next generation
 coating ­material20, has not yet been investigated in SIS structures. The development of SIS structures requires
 overcoming many materials science and technological challenges to achieve good superconducting properties
 and SRF performance while providing optimal stoichiometry and morphology of the layers and the interfaces
 and transparency of grain boundaries to extremely high RF current densities. In this work we report results on
 growth and characterizations of ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers which exhibit good superconducting properties and
 low-fi ld SRF performance on par with the cavity-grade Nb.

 Results and discussion
 Multilayer growth. We developed a technique of high-temperature confocal sputtering of Nb and Sn from
 elemental targets to grow stoichiometric N­ b3Sn multilayers with A­ l2O3 interlayers. Details are given in the Sup-
 plemental Information. Thin films and multilayers of different thicknesses were grown on different sapphire
 single crystal substrates for the subsequent characterizations. For instance, 60 nm thick ­Nb3Sn films were grown
 on 10 × 10 mm sapphire substrates for transport, scanning tunneling spectroscopy and electron microscopy
 characterizations. For RF tests, we grew ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 multilayers on 2″ diameter sapphire wafers (R-plane,
 300 µm thick). These multilayers had up to three 60 nm ­Nb3Sn layers separated by 6 nm ­Al2O3. The thickness of
 the ­Nb3Sn layers was chosen to be smaller than the London penetration ­depth5,6. A 200 nm thick Nb film was
 deposited on the backside of the wafers to prevent leakage of RF fi ld during cavity measurements. The geometry
 of multilayer samples used in our RF measurements of quality factors is shown in Fig. 1.
 The Nb-Sn phase diagram contains several line compounds. For instance, N ­ b3Sn and N­ b6Sn5 coexist in the
 region marked in Fig. 2a. Here a low-Tc ­Nb6Sn5 phase is clearly undesirable in these fi ­ lms21. Within the ­Nb3Sn
 phase region extending from 17 to 25% Sn, the critical temperature T ­ c degrades steeply as stoichiometry moves
 away from a 3:1 r­ atio22. These two conditions demand that N ­ b3Sn films should contain 25% of Sn. Th s was
 accomplished by providing processing conditions reflecting the field in the upper right of the phase diagram
 in Fig. 2a, a two-phase region containing only stoichiometric N  ­ b3Sn and liquid Sn. Films were grown by con-
 focal sputtering of Nb and Sn from elemental targets. By providing a large over-pressure of Sn at high growth
 temperatures, it has been found that the ratio of Nb:Sn can be pinned at 3:1. The abundance of Sn drives the

          Scientific Reports |   (2021) 11:7770 |                https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-021-87119-9                                                      2

Vol:.(1234567890)

 Figure 2.  Connecting the Nb-Sn phase diagram to electrical properties and process window. (a) Relevant
 adsorption-controlled growth regime (orange) of Nb-Sn phase diagram. ­Nb3Sn spans 17–25% Sn, and the
 desired ­Nb3Sn + liquid Sn fi ld lies above 930 °C and 25% Sn. (b) ­Tc, ∆Tc, vs the Sn flux of 60 nm thick ­Nb3Sn
 single layer thin films on ­Al2O3 substrates. ­Tc levels off bove 1.0 Å/s Sn, corresponding to the adsorption-
 controlled growth window and the film composition reach 25% Sn.

 material into the two-phase region, where excess Sn re-evaporates from the film, avoiding the formation of Sn
 ­precipitates23,24. To achieve the high temperatures (> 930 °C) required for this growth, sapphire substrates were
 heated from behind with a SiC radiative heater. Radiation passed through the substrate and heated the deposit-
 ing metal directly. Growth temperature was measured by pyrometer. Details of the film growth are given in the
 Supplemental Information.
 A series of films was grown with fi ed Nb flux (0.7 Å/s) and varying Sn flux (0.4–2.5 Å/s), and low-tempera-
 ture resistance measurements were carried out to find the window for this self-regulating adsorption-controlled
 process. Shown in Fig. 2b are the dependencies of the critical temperature Tc and transition width ∆Tc on the
 deposition rate of Sn which clearly saturate at ~ 1 Å/s. Given the dependence of Tc on Sn content in ­Nb3Sn, this
 growth rate roughly corresponds to the boundary between two processing regimes. At lower flux, Sn evaporates
 from the film faster than it can be incorporated, resulting in a Sn-deficient film. At higher flux, sufficient Sn is
 provided to react with all available Nb, and only excess Sn re-evaporates.
 The dielectric A ­ l2O3 interlayers were grown after allowing N ­ b3Sn to cool down to < 400 °C, using a single
 stoichiometric target with RF power at a rate of 1.8 nm/min without any further heating applied to the substrate.
 Depositing under these conditions protects the SiC heater element from oxygen evolved during the sputtering
 process and prevents undesired reactions with the N ­ b3Sn surface. Th s N
 ­ b3Sn/Al2O3 stack was then heated again
 to above 900 °C, which allows the A ­ l2O3 to crystallize, and the process was repeated to grow heterostructures of
 up to three ­Nb3Sn layers. The chamber setup and growth steps are depicted in Fig. 3.

 Structural characterization. A SIS sample with three ­Nb3Sn layers was prepared for analysis by cross-
 sectional scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM). A low-magnifi ation image (Fig. 4a) represents
 the morphology and nanostructure of the stack. Each N ­ b3Sn layer is polycrystalline with irregular interfaces and
 grain size is 20–100 nm along the film surface direction. The ­Al2O3 layers conform closely to the layer below
 but are discontinuous along the N  ­ b3Sn/Al2O3 interface. Despite the repeated thermal cycling during stacking, it
 appears that the lower layers have not degraded in comparison to the top layer.
 The chemical stability of these films is further confirmed by compositional mapping with energy dispersive
 spectroscopy (EDS) (Fig. 4b). Intensity of the Al Kα and O Kα peaks are mapped from the region shown on the
 left. Although the ­Al2O3 layers are not continuous, Al and O are confi ed to the ­Al2O3 layers, and do not mix with
 the ­Nb3Sn layers. The exception to this is the presence of O at the interface of the topmost ­Nb3Sn film with the
 atmosphere, where ambient conditions are suffici t to cause a reaction. A capping ­Al2O3 layer could be deposited

 Figure 3.  Schematic of thin films deposition setup and sequential processing steps for N ­ b3Sn/Al2O3 multilayer
 heterostructures. (a) Nb and Sn are sputtered onto ­Al2O3 substrate while heater element is powered on. (b) After
 allowing film to cool, ­Al2O3 is sputtered from a single stoichiometric ­Al2O3 target. Sample is heated again to
 anneal ­Al2O3. These two steps are repeated to produce multilayer samples.

 interdiffusion between ­Al2O3 and ­Nb3Sn. (c) High-magnifi ation image of the interfaces between ­Al2O3 and
 ­Nb3Sn.

 to prevent this oxidation, but we did not use it for the multilayer samples described in this work. Note that the
 slight O signal in the ­Nb3Sn layers in Fig. 4b are due to the slight oxidation of the TEM specimen surface. As our
 RF cavity measurements show, these ­Al2O3 layers do not contribute signifi antly to surface resistance at low fi lds.
 A higher-magnifi ation image of the S–I interface is shown in Fig. 4c. The atomic structure of N ­ b3Sn is well-
 preserved at the interface, suggesting that there is almost no diffusion or intermixing from the A ­ l2O3. The lower
 ­Nb3Sn grain orients the [023] direction normal to the film surface, and this direction is also preserved in the
 upper ­Nb3Sn grain. This can occur when the upper N ­ b3Sn layer deposits with the same epitaxial relationship to
 the underlying A ­ l2O3 as the lower layer has with the A
 ­ l2O3 substrate. Th s structure can also form when a N ­ b3Sn

 Superconducting properties. Our dc transport measurements have shown that the ­Nb3Sn films capped
 with ­Al2O3 and annealed with no further deposition exhibit good superconducting properties. For instance, the
 superconducting resistive transitions of a bare ­Nb3Sn film and a ­Nb3Sn/Al2O3 structure annealed at 900 °C for
 10 min are shown in Fig. 5a. Here the critical temperature of the annealed sample is about 0.25 K higher than Tc
 of the unannealed sample, and residual resistivity ratio (RRR), an indicator of crystalline and metallic quality, is
 improved from 3.5 to 4.26. On the other hand, N  ­ b3Sn films annealed without the ­Al2O3 cap, even under high Sn
 flux to prevent evaporative loss, have degraded superconducting properties compared to an un-annealed film.
 The superconducting properties essential for the RF performance were characterized by scanning tunneling
 spectroscopy (STS) which measures the differential tunneling conductance dI/dV proportional to the quasi-
 particle density of states (DOS), N(E). Shown in Fig. 5b is a representative tunneling spectrum measured in the
 center of a ­Nb3Sn grain at 4 K. The DOS curves, which clearly show the superconducting gap  at the Fermi
 surface, were fit using the conventional Dynes ­model25,26:

 consistent with the conventional gap value for a stoichiometric ­Nb3Sn4. The ratio Ŵ/� ≈ 13% in our samples
 turns out to be about 2–3 times larger than the values observed by tunneling spectroscopy on 1–2 µm thick

 low energies E < � may indicate the effects of local non-stoichiometry, gap anisotropy and s­ train22, scattering
 of quasiparticles on magnetic impurities, and a thin layer with deteriorated superconducting properties at the
                                            ­surface27–30. In turn, the subgap quasiparticles states which appear at |E|< ∆ due to a fin te Γ contribute to a
 temperature-independent residual surface resistance Ri at kBT <  < ∆5,29
